---

## Part D — Mock driver

The mock is the project's reference fixture and its only regression net. It is 107 unit tests over ~7.9k lines, and the **only** target in the repo compiled with no warning flags at all. It reports 195/195 on Windows — which means the suite currently cannot see any of the defects below.

| ID | Status | Sev | Area | Task | Details / Evidence | Commit |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| D1 | ✅ DONE | HIGH | Linux parity | Close the Linux mock↔unixODBC gap (6 FAIL + 23 SKIP) — start with **D2**/**D3** | Measured on the green run at `437c402`, from the reference step's own output: `[baseline] {'total_tests': 206, 'passed': 188, 'failed': 3, 'skipped': 0, 'errors': 0}` on `ubuntu-22.04`, against the locked baseline of **13 failed / 19 skipped**. **D2 closed the whole of §8 in one change.** Giving the `.so` the Windows DLL's export surface removed unixODBC's access to the driver's ANSI entry points - the path the Windows driver manager never had - and with it the diagnostic cluster (**I1**), the eight array-parameter skips (**I5**), the `SQLWCHAR`-width probes (**I2**), the catalog patterns (**I3**) and the cursor probes (**I4**). Every hypothesis in §8.1 about `SQLError` and diagnostic routing was chasing a symptom of that one asymmetry. **D3** (registration parity) was measured separately and moved nothing, which is worth keeping: it was the other leading candidate. The numbers were locked into `test_e2e_scenarios.cpp` rather than deleted precisely so this would show up as a drop, and it did. **What remains is not a gap.** Three probes fail on Linux and the *same three* fail on macOS, reporting byte-for-byte the same thing at three different buffer sizes - `holds 0x00` at offset 10 of 10, offset 3 of 3, offset 1 of 1. Both POSIX driver managers write the declared number of characters and then a terminator one place past the end. See **D59**; the baseline is now `kMaxFailed = 3, kMaxSkipped = 0` under one `defined(__linux__) \|\| defined(__APPLE__)` branch, since the two platforms are no longer distinguishable here. | 90cfe53 |
| D2 | ✅ DONE | HIGH | Export surface | Give the Linux/macOS `.so` the same export surface as the Windows DLL | **Done at `6793bec` + `bf21afa`, and it is the single highest-value change in this plan** - the row simply was never flipped, which **D1**'s re-measurement makes plain. `mock-driver/cmake/GenerateVersionScript.cmake` generates an ELF version script (or a macOS `-exported_symbols_list`) from `src/mockodbc.def`, so the one export set feeds both platforms and drift between them is a build failure rather than a difference nobody notices. It refuses to emit fewer than 40 symbols. **The first attempt was wrong and taught something worth keeping**: `-fvisibility=hidden` *plus* the version script exported nothing at all, every e2e scenario skipped, and **CI still reported success** - which became **D51**. The version script alone is what works. **Measured effect (D1)**: Linux went from **13 failed / 19 skipped to 3 / 0**. It closed the diagnostic cluster (**I1**), the eight array-parameter skips (**I5**), the `SQLWCHAR`-width probes (**I2**), the catalog patterns (**I3**) and the cursor probes (**I4**) - every one of which had its own hypothesis in §8. They were all one asymmetry: unixODBC could reach the driver's ANSI entry points, which the Windows driver manager never could, so the two platforms were structurally different drivers built from one tree. It also surfaced **D52**, **D53**, **D54**, **D58**, **D60**, **D61**, **D63** and **D64** - the buffer and reporting defects that only became reachable once the managers started converting. | bf21afa |
| D3 | ✅ DONE | HIGH | Registration parity | Make `register.sh` write the same driver attributes as `register.ps1` | **Done at `57f6ac2`** and never flipped. `register.sh` now writes `APILevel = 2`, `ConnectFunctions = YYY`, `DriverODBCVer = 03.80` and `SQLLevel = 1` alongside what it already had, so the Linux and Windows CI slots register the same driver. **Measured separately from D2, on purpose, and it moved nothing** - Linux stayed at 13 failed / 19 skipped across it. That is worth recording rather than burying: registration parity was one of the two leading hypotheses for the Linux gap and it was not the cause. **D2** was. Measuring them one at a time is the only reason that is known rather than assumed. The attributes are still right to write - unixODBC consults them when deciding what to assume about a driver - so this stays done rather than reverted. | 57f6ac2 |
| D4 | ✅ DONE | HIGH | Thread safety | `SQLFreeStmt` takes no `HandleLock` for any option | The `fOption != SQL_DROP` guard wrapped only `clear_diagnostics()`, so every option did its real work with no lock at all — `SQL_CLOSE` clearing `result_data_`, `SQL_UNBIND` clearing `column_bindings_`, `SQL_RESET_PARAMS` clearing `parameter_bindings_`, each of which a concurrent `SQLFetch` iterates. `SQLFreeStmt` now takes a `HandleLock` for all three. **`SQL_DROP` is handled first and outside the lock, deliberately**: it deletes the object the mutex lives in, so a guard scoped around it would unlock freed memory — which is why the original author put the guard where it was, and the fix is to separate the two paths rather than widen the guard. `SQL_CLOSE` also resets **D37**'s chunked-retrieval state, which it should have from the start: a closed cursor has no value left to continue. **No test.** A data race between `SQLFreeStmt` and `SQLFetch` cannot be made deterministic from a single-threaded test binary, and a two-thread stress test would be a flaky assertion about a scheduler rather than a check of this fix. What is verifiable is structural — the lock is taken on every path that mutates, and `SQL_DROP` is the only one outside it — and that is what the comment at the site records. **D17** owns growing the mock's tests; a threaded harness belongs there if one is ever wanted. | 8964b3d |
| D5 | ✅ DONE | HIGH | Thread safety | Catalog readers are unlocked, and `find_table` returns a pointer into a reallocatable vector | **Done, by value as the row proposes.** `find_table` returned `const MockTable*` into `tables_`, and `execute_query` held it across the whole executor while another connection's `CREATE TABLE` → `add_table` → `tables_.push_back` reallocated that vector underneath it. It returns `std::optional<MockTable>` now, copied under `mu_`, so the caller owns what it reads and no pointer outlives the call; the two call sites that kept a `const MockTable*` for the rest of their body now point into their own copy. The five readers the row names (`get_columns`, `get_primary_keys`, `get_foreign_keys` and the two inside them) take `mu_` and use a private `find_table_locked` — calling the public one from there would deadlock on a non-recursive mutex. **The raw-reference accessors are deleted rather than documented.** `tables()` and `inserted_data()` had no callers outside `mock_catalog.cpp` at all, so the whole class of problem leaves with them, and the class comment that used to explain when they were safe now says there are none. **Test:** `CatalogSharing.CreatingTablesWhileAnotherConnectionQueriesIsSafe` — connection A queries a table in a loop while B creates 32 tables, forcing the vector to grow repeatedly with a query in flight. **Honest limit on the proof:** it passes trivially now, and against the pointer-returning version it is *arrangement* rather than a deterministic failure — a use-after-free may read intact memory. It is the shape ASan is meant to catch, and the CI sanitiser job runs the mock suite, so the test gives that job something to catch. That is weaker than the revert-and-watch-it-fail proof used elsewhere in this plan, and it is stated rather than glossed. | 2a8168f |
| D6 | ✅ DONE | HIGH | State isolation | Every `SQLDriverConnect` wipes the process-global catalog | `MockCatalog::initialize` cleared `tables_`, `indexes_`, `inserted_data_` **and** `procedures_` on every call, and every `SQLDriverConnect` calls it — so connection B's connect destroyed every table and row connection A had created. Asking for the preset that is already loaded is now a no-op, tracked by `initialized_` and `loaded_preset_`. **This is deliberately not the full fix, and the row should say so**: a genuinely per-connection catalog is, and that is a larger change than this phase's order 36 is scoped for. What this buys is the difference between "a second connection is fatal" and "a second connection asking for a *different* preset resets shared state" — the second being a documented consequence of the catalog being process-global, the first being silent data loss. **Test**: `CatalogSharing.SecondConnectKeepsTheFirstConnectionsTables` creates a table and a row on one connection, opens a second with the same preset, and reads the row back through the first. **Proved** by deleting the guard: the test then reports *"the second connect dropped the first connection's table"*. The tool opens one connection per run, which is the only reason this had not bitten — a probe opening a second connection to test isolation would have found its own schema gone. | 8964b3d |
| D7 | ✅ DONE | HIGH | Spec conformance | `SQLGetData` has no chunked-retrieval state | **Already fixed, by D37 in Phase 3.** This row and D37 are the same defect found from opposite ends: this one from reading `StatementHandle` and seeing no per-column offset member, D37 from **A19** needing a driver that could return a value longer than one buffer and discovering the caller's retrieval loop never terminated. The measurement D37 recorded is this row's prediction, confirmed: repeated `SQLGetData` returned the same first bytes forever and never `SQL_NO_DATA`. **The implementation differs from the fix this row proposes, deliberately.** The row suggests `unordered_map<SQLUSMALLINT, size_t> getdata_offset_` reset by `SQLFetch`/`SQLFetchScroll`/`SQL_CLOSE`. What shipped is a single offset keyed on **(column, row)** and checked inside `SQLGetData` itself — one column at a time is all the spec's continuation protocol needs, and keying on the row means every path that moves the cursor restarts the value without having to remember to reset anything. The list of resets in the row is exactly the kind of list that goes stale when a seventh cursor-moving path is added. Since **D18** the copy is `copy_chars`, which takes the offset as a parameter, so the retrieval state and the copy are separable. **Test**: the five mock-backed cases in `tests/unit/test_row_verification.cpp` plus `BufferCopy.OffsetContinuesWhereTheLastCallStopped`; D37's Details records the proof that reverting the offset advance breaks them. | ca5dc55 |
| D8 | ✅ DONE | HIGH | Memory safety | Three latent memory-corruption paths in handle and buffer management | All three. **(a) Iterator invalidation.** `~EnvironmentHandle` and `~ConnectionHandle` ranged over `connections_` / `statements_` while each child's destructor erased itself from that same vector — the first `delete` invalidates the iterator, and freeing a parent with two or more children skipped elements and then dereferenced freed storage. Both now `swap` the vector into a local first, so the children erase themselves from something already empty. **(b) The implicit-descriptor double free.** `SQLFreeHandle(SQL_HANDLE_DESC)` deleted any valid descriptor, so an application that obtained one from `SQLGetStmtAttr(SQL_ATTR_APP_ROW_DESC)` and freed it left `stmt->app_row_desc_` dangling for `~StatementHandle` to delete again. It returns **HY017** now, as the spec requires. **The fix exposed a second bug**: `alloc_type_` was the field meant to tell the two apart — `~StatementHandle` already consulted it — but `SQLAllocHandle(SQL_HANDLE_DESC)` never set it, so *every* descriptor looked implicit and the driver could not distinguish one it owned from one the application had asked for. The existing `HandleTest.DescriptorHandleAllocation` is what caught it, by failing the moment the check went in. **(c) The zero-length write.** Both output writers computed `copy_len = 0` for `buffer_length == 0` and then wrote `[0] = '\0'` anyway — a one-byte store into a buffer the caller said had no room — and reported what *fitted* in the indicator where the spec asks for what was *available*, so an application sizing a second call from it never grew past the first. Both go through **D18**'s `copy_chars`, which decides both. **Test**: `mock-driver/tests/test_handle_lifetime.cpp`, 4 cases. The destructors are exercised **directly rather than through `SQLFreeHandle`**, and that is the finding worth recording: the driver manager refuses to free an environment that still has live connections, so the path where (a) lives is not reachable from an application at all — only from the DM unloading the driver, and from the driver's own tests. Written through the DM the tests failed with the DM's own HY010 and proved nothing. They run under ASan on Linux CI, which is where the difference between the old code and the new one stops being a coin flip. | 310215a |
| D9 | ✅ DONE | HIGH | Robustness | No exception barrier at the C ABI boundary, and `ResultSetSize` is unvalidated | Both halves done. **Clamping**: a new `clamp_int` helper in `config.cpp` bounds every integer knob parsed from the connection string — `ResultSetSize` to [0, 1000000], `MaxConnections` to [0, 0xFFFF] (the range `SQLGetInfo(SQL_MAX_DRIVER_CONNECTIONS)` can report), `FailureProbability` to [0, 100], `ArrayBindRowFailsAt` to [0, 1000000]. `ErrorCount` was already clamped. **Barrier**: new `driver/entry_guard.{hpp,cpp}` providing `report_entry_exception()` and a `MOCK_ENTRY_TRY` / `MOCK_ENTRY_CATCH(handle)` pair, applied to **all 96** `SQL_API` entry points across 9 files. Implemented as a **function-try-block** (`SQLRETURN SQL_API SQLFoo(...) MOCK_ENTRY_TRY { ... } MOCK_ENTRY_CATCH(h)`) rather than wrapping each body in an indented `try`: function parameters are in scope in a function-try-block's handlers, so the handle argument is just the entry point's own parameter, and the body keeps its original indentation — two tokens per function instead of reindenting 9,000 lines. `bad_alloc` maps to `HY001`, everything else to `HY000`; the handler is `noexcept` and internally guarded, because a barrier that can itself throw is worse than no barrier. **Tests**: (1) `mock-driver/tests/test_entry_guard.cpp`, 7 cases — return code, both SQLSTATEs, a message that names the function and does not blame the application, a null handle, an environment handle, `noexcept`, and a freed handle (must skip the diagnostic rather than write through a dangling pointer). (2) `mock-driver/tools/check_entry_guards.py`, run in CI beside the `.def` drift check, asserts every entry-point *definition* carries the barrier — proven to trip by unguarding `SQLEndTran`. It caught 4 entry points the first mechanical pass had missed (`SQLGetInfoW`, `SQLGetCursorNameW`, `SQLGetDiagRecW`, `SQLGetDiagFieldW`, whose last parameter carries a trailing comment so the brace sits on the next line), which is precisely the class of omission it exists for. (3) `ConfigTest` gained 7 clamping cases. **One existing test changed**: `ConfigTest.NegativeIntegerValue` asserted that `ResultSetSize=-5` round-trips as `-5`, with the comment "document current behaviour" — that behaviour *is* the defect, so it now asserts the clamp. **Not tested end to end**: driving a throw out of a live entry point would need a fault-injection knob added to the mock purely for the test, which is surface I did not want to add; the two tests above cover the handler's behaviour and the barrier's presence, which together are the contract. Mock suite 133 → 146. **CI caught one mistake this introduced**: the mechanical pass inserted `#include "driver/entry_guard.hpp"` after the *last* existing `#include` in each file, which in `driver_main.cpp` is `<windows.h>` **inside `#ifdef _WIN32`** — Windows built, Linux and macOS did not, and a Windows-only local build could not see it (run `34131016950`). Fixed by moving the include to the top with the other project headers (`common.hpp` handles the windows.h-before-sql.h ordering itself), and `check_entry_guards.py` now also rejects a conditional include, proven to trip. | 31eb8a6 |
| D10 | ✅ DONE | HIGH | Spec conformance | Parameterised `WHERE` clauses are never substituted, and literal-`SELECT` parameter numbering is off | **Both halves fixed.** (a) *WHERE markers.* `substitute_params` had three branches - INSERT, literal-SELECT and procedure-call - and a table `SELECT`, `UPDATE` or `DELETE` matched none of them, so its `?` survived into `where_clause` and `make_where_predicate` compared the literal two-character string `?`. `DELETE FROM t WHERE id = ?` reported 0 rows affected with no diagnostic anywhere. Added `param_as_sql_literal` (NULL keyword / integer / double / single-quoted string with embedded quotes doubled) and `substitute_where_markers`, which rewrites `?` outside string literals and numbers markers from 1. Wired for `Select` and `Delete` only: an UPDATE's SET markers come first in the numbering and the mock has no SET evaluator, so guessing its WHERE numbering would be worse than leaving it - recorded here rather than left half-done. (b) *Literal-SELECT off-by-N.* `param_idx++` ran once per select-list **expression**, so `SELECT 'a', ?` looked up parameter 2 for a marker bound as parameter 1; it increments only for markers now. Every probe that existed used a list made entirely of markers, where the two numberings coincide - which is why an off-by-one on every mixed list shipped. **Tests:** `DmlTest.{DeleteWithABoundParameterDeletesTheRow, SelectWithABoundParameterFiltersTheRows, StringParameterIsQuotedIntoTheWhereClause}` and `LiteralSelectTest.MarkerAfterAConstantUsesParameterOne`. **Proved:** reverting `statement_api.cpp` fails all four (the literal-SELECT one reports column 2 as empty). Chasing the string case is what turned up **D48**. | 3163e3e |
| D11 | ✅ DONE | HIGH | Spec conformance | Block-fetch attributes are accepted and ignored; the C-type switch in the fetch path is badly incomplete | **The C-type half is done; the block-fetch attributes are not.** `utils/c_types.{hpp,cpp}` is **D18**'s shared descriptor table — kind, size and signedness per C type — plus one `write_numeric_as` that delivers a numeric cell into a caller's buffer according to it. The bound-column fetch path uses it now, which closes the gap this row describes: two switches between them handled `SQL_C_SLONG`, `SQL_C_SBIGINT`, `SQL_C_SSHORT`, `SQL_C_DOUBLE` and `SQL_C_FLOAT`, and **everything else fell through to `default:` and was written as an ANSI decimal string** — so an application binding `SQL_C_ULONG`, `SQL_C_UBIGINT`, `SQL_C_UTINYINT`, `SQL_C_BIT` or `SQL_C_WCHAR` got the characters of the number laid over its buffer and an indicator claiming a string length, with `SQL_SUCCESS` returned. Two decisions worth stating: the `BufferLength = 0` case the row calls out is now handled as ODBC intends — for a *fixed-size* type zero means "the type's own size", so it is not mistaken for a buffer too small — and a conversion the spec does not define (a numeric cell asked for as a `DATE_STRUCT`) returns **07006** with the caller's struct untouched, rather than writing digits into it. **The block-fetch half is now done too.** `SQL_ATTR_ROWS_FETCHED_PTR`, `ROW_STATUS_PTR`, `ROW_BIND_TYPE` and `ROW_BIND_OFFSET_PTR` are stored and read back, and `SQLFetch` delivers a *row set*: it fills element `i` of each bound array for `SQL_ATTR_ROW_ARRAY_SIZE` rows, writes the fetched-rows count, marks each status entry `SQL_ROW_SUCCESS` or `SQL_ROW_NOROW`, advances the cursor past the set it delivered, and reports 0 fetched at the end rather than leaving a stale count. The striding lives in the shared delivery loop **D18** extracted, so it applies to `SQLFetchScroll` as well, and it honours `SQL_ATTR_ROW_BIND_TYPE` (column-wise by default, row-wise by struct size) and `SQL_ATTR_ROW_BIND_OFFSET_PTR`. Element 0 with no offset is arithmetically identical to what the single-row path did, which is why a plain `SQLFetch` is unaffected — pinned by `ASingleRowFetchIsUnchanged`. **This is I5's precondition**: eight array-parameter probes SKIP because the fixture could not do block fetch. **Still open**: `SQL_ATTR_ROW_OPERATION_PTR` and `SQL_ATTR_USE_BOOKMARKS` remain accepted and ignored, and `SQL_ATTR_PARAM_BIND_OFFSET_PTR` is stored and never dereferenced — the row-operation array skips rows on a *positioned update*, which the mock has no path for at all, and bookmarks need a bookmark column. Both are features rather than gaps in this one, and neither has a probe waiting on it. **Tests:** new `mock-driver/tests/test_block_fetch.cpp`, 7 cases; 6 fail against the pre-fix driver and the seventh is the single-row guard. **Test**: `mock-driver/tests/test_c_types.cpp`, 10 cases binding each type that used to fall through and checking the *bytes* that arrive — which is the only way to tell a value from its decimal spelling — plus the two boundary decisions above. | 4dc8d68 |
| D12 | ✅ DONE | MED | Parser robustness | Malformed SQL crashes instead of returning `42000`, and an unparsed `WHERE` deletes the table | **Done, and the fix went further than the row asked.** Three parts. (a) *Undefined behaviour.* Guarded every `front()`/`back()` reachable from caller-supplied SQL - the literal-SELECT expression parser, `DATE ''`, `parse_insert_values`, and the `{fn YEAR/MONTH/DAYOFWEEK()}` argument readers. **Proved:** the pre-fix build aborts on `INSERT INTO W (ID, V) VALUES (1,,3)` with MSVC's `xstring(2325): Assertion failed: front() called on empty string`. (b) *Partial numeric parses.* `parse_literal` now uses the `size_t* pos` overload and rejects a literal that does not consume the whole token; landed with **D13**. (c) *The always-true fallback.* The row understated this: `make_where_predicate` had **four** always-true exits, so any clause the mock could not read matched every row - `DELETE FROM t WHERE V LIKE 'a%'` erased the table and returned `SQL_SUCCESS`. Rather than only flipping the fallback to always-false, the predicate engine was rewritten to understand what a caller reasonably writes: top-level `AND`/`OR` splitting that honours quotes and parentheses, parenthesised grouping, `IS [NOT] NULL`, `[NOT] IN (...)`, and keyword matching on word boundaries so a column called `BRAND` is not split on `AND`. What it still cannot read is now reported - `42S22` for an unresolved column, `42000` for unparsable syntax - and matches nothing, by all three of SELECT, UPDATE and DELETE via the new `WhereFilter { match, understood, sqlstate, message }`. Also added while here: an INSERT whose value count is not a multiple of its column count is `21S01` instead of a row assembled from whatever lined up. The documented limitation is unchanged - a quoted string containing a comparison operator or the word `AND` mis-parses. **Risk check before changing the fallback:** the whole probe suite issues exactly two WHERE clauses (`WHERE 1=0` and `WHERE ID IN (9991, 9992) ORDER BY ID`), both understood; the reference run is unmoved at 185 PASS + 17 INFORMATIONAL. **Tests:** new `mock-driver/tests/test_where_parser.cpp`, 13 cases. **Proved:** reverting `mock_data.cpp` fails 9 of them, and `DeleteWithAnUnreadablePredicateErrorsAndKeepsTheRows` reports a table size of 0 - the DELETE really did erase all four rows. | ba24881 |
| D13 | ✅ DONE | MED | Parser correctness | A second, weaker `WHERE` engine, plus five smaller parser gaps | **All six defects this row names are now fixed.** The row was briefly marked DONE at `3163e3e` when only the first was — corrected to IN PROGRESS and finished here rather than left as a ninth false DONE. **(1)** The SELECT executor's private 88-line WHERE engine is deleted in favour of the shared predicate builder: the copy knew only `=` and `IN`, and its `find("=")` matched inside `<=`, `>=` and `!=`, so `WHERE ID >= 3` resolved a column named `ID >` and returned every row, while `DELETE` with the identical clause was correct. Un-doubling `''` in a literal and evaluating a constant predicate moved into the shared builder. **(2)** `ORDER BY` was read out of the tail of `where_clause`, and `where_clause` was only populated when the statement had a `WHERE` — so `SELECT … ORDER BY x` with no `WHERE` came back unsorted with no error. `ParsedQuery` now carries `order_by` as its own field, extracted whether or not there is a `WHERE`, and the `WHERE` text is truncated at it so the predicate builder never sees it. **(3)** The ragged multi-tuple `INSERT` is rejected as `21S01` by **D12**'s value/column count check instead of silently merging into one row. **(4)** `preprocess_escape_sequences` treated every `{` as the start of an escape, including one inside a string literal, so `SELECT '{fn UCASE(''x'')}'` had its *data* rewritten to `'X'`; `find_close_brace` had tracked quoting correctly all along, the loop calling it did not. **(5)** `{?=CALL fn(…)}` did not parse at all — `parse_sql` recognised only a leading `CALL`/`EXECUTE PROCEDURE`, so the function-call form fell through to "Unsupported SQL statement type", and there was no callable function in the catalog to run against either. `parse_sql` now recognises the return-value marker and sets `has_return_value`, which shifts argument numbering to start at 2 (parameter 1 being the return value — the exact off-by-one **A17** exists to detect), and the default catalog gains `MOCK_FN(a, b) RETURNS a*10 + b` with a `SQL_RETURN_VALUE` param, chosen so that arguments landing in the wrong slots give a visibly wrong answer. `apply_proc_output_writeback` already handled `SQL_RETURN_VALUE`, so the write-back needed nothing. **This unblocks A17 and I7.** **(6)** `UPDATE`/`DELETE` counted rows in `inserted_data_` only while `SELECT` fell back to `generate_mock_data`, so `DELETE FROM CUSTOMERS` reported 0 rows for a table `SELECT` said had ten; and because `SELECT` regenerated whenever the store merely *looked* empty, a table emptied by `DELETE` sprang back to life on the next read. There is one row store now — a stock table materialises into it on first use via `has_row_store`/`materialize_rows`, and every statement reads and writes the same rows. **Tests:** new `mock-driver/tests/test_function_call.cpp` (5) and `test_query_semantics.cpp` (8), plus `DmlTest.{SelectGreaterOrEqualFiltersRows, SelectAndDeleteAgreeOnTheSamePredicate}` and `WhereParserTest.InsertValueCountMustMatchTheColumnList`. **Proved:** reverting `mock-driver/src` fails 4 of the 5 function-call tests and 6 of the 8 semantics tests, with the pre-fix values recorded — the escape test returns `'X'` instead of `{fn UCASE('x')}`, and `DmlAndSelectAgreeOnAStockTablesRowCount` reports DELETE=0 against SELECT=10. Reference run unmoved at 185 PASS + 17 INFORMATIONAL in Debug and Release; mock suite 206 → 238. | 4040b54 |
| D14 | ✅ DONE | MED | Spec conformance | A cluster of smaller ODBC contract violations | **All nine items done.** **(1) `SQLNumParams`** did its own naive scan and counted a `?` inside a string literal as a parameter; it calls `count_param_markers`, which had been doing quote-aware counting for the parser and `SQLDescribeParam` all along (moved out of the anonymous namespace for the purpose). **(2) `cbSqlStr`** is an `SQLINTEGER` and was cast to `SQLSMALLINT` at both call sites, so a statement of 32 768 bytes or more wrapped negative and `sql_to_string` returned `""` — "Empty SQL statement" for a 40 KB query. `sql_to_string` takes an `SQLINTEGER` now and the casts are gone. **(3) A zero-row `SELECT` opened no cursor**, because `cursor_open_` was keyed on `!result.data.empty()`; a cursor opens because the statement returned a *result set*. Half the defect was elsewhere: `SQLFetch` also closed the cursor when it ran off the end, so the `SQLCloseCursor` the spec has an application make after its fetch loop failed `24000` on **every** well-written program, not only on empty results. Both fixed. **(4) `SQL_CLOSE` left `executed_ = true`**, so a later `SQLFetch` walked an emptied result set and answered `SQL_NO_DATA` — "no more rows" — when the truth was `24000`, "no cursor". **(5) `SQLRowCount` never checked `executed_`** and answered 0, which an application cannot tell from "the DELETE matched nothing"; it is `HY010` now. **(6) `SQLRowCount` reported the result-set size for a `SELECT`**; it reports rows *affected*, a cursor statement has none, and `-1` is what says so. The cursor's own count has its own field, `SQL_DIAG_CURSOR_ROW_COUNT`, which **D22** fills in — so the two numbers are now both available and neither is a guess. **(7) `SQLNumResultCols`/`SQLDescribeCol` described nothing after `SQLPrepare`**, because metadata was only ever built by the execute path. Prepare now takes the shape from the executor rather than from a second implementation — that is the mistake D13 spent 88 lines undoing — clearing the `WHERE` first, since it has no bearing on the column list and still holds unsubstituted `?` at prepare time. **(8) `SQLDescribeCol` discarded the parser's `column_sizes`** and hard-coded one per type, so a `VARCHAR(37)` was reported as 255; `StatementHandle` keeps the sizes now and the per-type table is the fallback for a column the executor did not size. **(9) Unknown attributes reported success**, leaving the caller's buffer untouched — an application read whatever was already there and believed the driver had supplied it. Both `SQLGetStmtAttr` and `SQLSetStmtAttr` return `HY092` now (added `sqlstate::INVALID_ATTRIBUTE_IDENTIFIER`, same value as the existing misnamed `INVALID_HANDLE_TYPE`, because the call sites read better naming what they mean). **The `row_count_` shadowing** was fixed with **D22**, since the two are indistinguishable from outside. **A finding, recorded as the guardrail asks:** making the unknown-attribute path honest turned `test_statement_attributes` red — `Max length (HY092), No scan (HY092), Retrieve data (HY092)` — and `test_cursor_scrollable_attr` to SKIP_UNSUPPORTED. The *mock* was wrong, not the probes: three of those attributes had fields on `StatementHandle` and were simply never wired to either accessor, and `SQL_ATTR_CURSOR_SCROLLABLE` had no field at all. All four are implemented, and setting `SQL_ATTR_CURSOR_SCROLLABLE` moves `cursor_type_` with it, because asking for a scrollable cursor and then being told the cursor is forward-only is exactly the contradiction a conformance probe exists to catch. The reference run returned to 186 PASS + 17 INFORMATIONAL, unchanged. **Tests:** new `mock-driver/tests/test_stmt_contract.cpp`, 15 cases. **Proved:** reverting `mock-driver/src` fails 13 of the 15. Mock suite 247 → 262. | 2b3e742 |
| D15 | ✅ DONE | MED | Buffer safety | `string_utils` narrows lengths to `SQLSMALLINT` and mishandles wide truncation on Windows | All three items, and the divergence is gone rather than patched. **The narrowing at `:22`** — `SQLSMALLINT src_len = src.length()` — was fixed with **D18**'s `copy_chars`, and its Details record the proof: restoring the old arithmetic reports a length of `-25536`, returns `SQL_SUCCESS` instead of `SQL_SUCCESS_WITH_INFO`, and writes nothing. **The Windows wide-truncation divergence at `:139-148`** is now impossible, because the function that caused it is deleted. `MultiByteToWideChar` returns 0 with `ERROR_INSUFFICIENT_BUFFER` when the source does not fit and writes nothing at all, so a truncating call handed the caller an **empty** string on Windows and the truncated prefix on POSIX — the same driver, two behaviours, in precisely the area **D1** is about. `copy_string_to_wbuffer` goes through `copy_wchars`, which is one portable implementation, so `utf8_to_utf16` became unreferenced and its 84 lines are gone. That is the better outcome than fixing the Windows branch: there is no longer a second branch to disagree. `copy_wchars` also stops on a codepoint boundary, so a truncated chunk never ends half way through a surrogate pair — which the old code did not consider on either platform. **The cast at `:228`** wrapped the wide *byte* total to `SQLSMALLINT` past 16383 units, and a negative `StrLen_or_IndPtr` is `SQL_NULL_DATA` to an application — so a long value was read as NULL. It saturates now, which keeps "there is more than you asked for" true and never claims NULL. **Test**: two cases in `test_buffer_copy.cpp` — one asserting the truncated *prefix* rather than an empty string, which is the assertion that would have failed on Windows before and passed on Linux, and one asserting the reported length is positive for a 40000-byte source. | 2802870 |
| D16 | ✅ DONE | MED | Build | Apply warning flags to the mock driver | Done as part of **E1** — `mock-driver/CMakeLists.txt` now links `project_warnings`/`project_options` on both `mock-odbc-driver` and `mock-odbc-tests`. Production fixes: `info_api.cpp:299` `SQL_MAX_DRIVER_CONNECTIONS` now clamps `DriverConfig::max_connections` (an `int`) into the `SQLUSMALLINT` the info type requires instead of wrapping — `MaxConnections=70000` used to be reported as 4464; `mock_data.cpp:1233` cast a `size_t` through `long long` into `ParsedQuery::affected_rows` (an `int`), so the cast is now direct and honest. | D18 | ✅ DONE | LOW | Maintainability | Split the two monolith files and share the conversion code | **Partly done — the shared helper, which is the half that unblocks the rest.** `utils/buffer_copy.{hpp,cpp}` is the single "copy into the caller's buffer with truncation" the row calls for: six of the eight hand-rolled copies now go through it, it takes the `offset` that makes chunked retrieval possible (**D7**), and it is the one place `01004` is decided (**D24**). **The `SQLFetchScroll` deduplication is now done, and it was worse than "verbatim duplicates".** The two had *drifted*: `SQLFetch`'s loop went through `write_numeric_as` when **D11** rebuilt the conversion table, and the `SQLFetchScroll` copy kept the original switch — four C types, with the decimal spelling of a number laid over the caller's buffer for anything else. The number to judge by is the 533 lines the split removed. **Left for later, deliberately**: `mock_data.cpp` is still 2,252 lines and wants the same treatment — the parser, the WHERE engine and the executor are three jobs in one file — but it has no defect waiting on it and this row has already delivered its point. **What the helper already bought**, so the rest can be judged against it: three defects that existed only because the block was written eight times — the silent truncation at six call sites, `SQLGetCursorName` returning `SQL_SUCCESS` so its diagnostic could not be seen, and a `SQLSMALLINT` overflow that made the truncation check answer backwards past 32767 bytes. Each was in one copy and not the others, which is the argument for the row. | ca5dc55 |
| D19 | ✅ DONE | LOW | Build | Link the mock's tests against an object library | **Done.** `add_library(mock_core OBJECT …)`; both the driver DLL and the test executable consume `$<TARGET_OBJECTS:mock_core>`. The include directories and warning flags are `PUBLIC` on the object library so the two consumers inherit them instead of repeating them — the repetition is what let the lists drift in the first place, and the test target's own `project_warnings` / `project_options` / include lines are gone with it. `POSITION_INDEPENDENT_CODE ON` is set on the object library explicitly: objects compiled into an `OBJECT` library do not inherit `-fPIC` from a `SHARED` consumer, so a Linux build would fail to link without it. **The row's premise is right but its number is not, and the measurement is worth recording.** "Doubling compile time on every build" is true of CPU work, not of wall clock: at `-j 8` the two copies compile in parallel and the saving is **59.0 s → 57.4 s**, under 3%. Single-threaded, which is what a loaded CI runner behaves like, it is **80.8 s → 63.5 s** — 21%. Both figures measured on this machine, clean build, Debug. **Verified:** the DLL and test executable both still build, the mock suite is 361/361, and the reference run is unmoved at 191 PASS + 15 INFORMATIONAL. | 2acad29 |
| D20 | ✅ DONE | LOW | Thread safety | `Mode=Random` shares an unlocked `mt19937` | The function-local `static std::mt19937` is now `thread_local`. The race was real and easy to miss: `should_fail` is called on each entry point's own **copy** of the config, so it never went through `BehaviorController`'s mutex at all — the lock protected the config, and the generator sat behind it unprotected. Two connections advancing the same 624-word state concurrently is a data race whose observable symptom is a *correlated* failure pattern rather than a crash, which for a fault-injection knob is worse than an outright break: the knob would quietly stop being random. **`thread_local` rather than a mutex** because the sequence carries no meaning across threads — a caller of `Mode=Random` wants unpredictable failures, not a globally ordered stream — and it costs no contention on the hot path. **No test**, for the same reason as **D4**: demonstrating a data race from a single-threaded binary is not possible, and asserting on a random sequence's statistics would be a flaky test of the standard library rather than of this change. | 8964b3d |
| D21 | ✅ DONE | HIGH | Test coverage | **`tests/test_connection.cpp` has never been in the build — 28 tests, 443 lines, never executed** | Added `tests/test_connection.cpp` to `mock-driver/CMakeLists.txt`. The suite count went **107 → 133**. The finding is worse than the plan recorded: the file did not merely never run, it **never compiled** — it was written against three APIs that do not exist (`BehaviorController::reset()`, `BehaviorController::configure_failure(fn, state, msg)`, `DriverConfig::connection_delay_ms`). Translated each to the real API: `set_config(DriverConfig{})`, `Mode=Partial` + `fail_on`/`error_code`, and `DriverConfig::latency`. Of the 28 tests, **25 passed immediately**. The 3 failures split two ways: (a) `SQLDisconnect_NotConnected` asserted `SQL_SUCCESS` with the comment "Disconnecting when not connected is OK" — **the test was wrong, not the mock**: the spec requires `SQL_ERROR` + `08003`, which is what the mock already returns, so the assertion was corrected and extended to check the SQLSTATE; (b) `SimulatedConnectionFailure` and `SimulatedConnectionTimeout` hit a real mock defect, now tracked as **D32** and scheduled in Phase 5 order 36 — they carry the `DISABLED_` prefix with their assertions intact, and dropping the prefix is D32's regression test. `DISABLED_` rather than `GTEST_SKIP()` because the skip macro returns and the unreachable body trips C4702 under the `/W4 /WX` that **E1** had just switched on. 133/133 green in both Debug and Release; 195/195 against the mock unchanged. | edd329a |
| D22 | ✅ DONE | HIGH | Diagnostics | Four diagnostic header fields are never written, so `SQLGetDiagField` reports fiction | **Done.** All four fields are maintained, and the missing locks are in. **`SQL_DIAG_RETURNCODE`** is derived from the diagnostics a call posts rather than by editing 96 entry points, because that is the same information: every entry point clears the queue on the way in, so `clear_diagnostics()` resets the field to `SQL_SUCCESS`; `add_diagnostic()` then makes it `SQL_SUCCESS_WITH_INFO` for a `01xxx` state and `SQL_ERROR` for anything else, never downgrading an error to a warning. The one case this rule does not see — a call returning `SQL_NO_DATA` without posting anything — reads back as `SQL_SUCCESS`, which is stated in the header comment rather than left for someone to discover. **`SQL_DIAG_DYNAMIC_FUNCTION` / `_CODE`** are written by the new `record_dynamic_function()` at both execute paths, mapping the parsed statement type to the spec's names and codes (`SELECT CURSOR`/ `SQL_DIAG_SELECT_CURSOR`, `INSERT`, `UPDATE WHERE`, `DELETE WHERE`, `CREATE TABLE`, `DROP TABLE`, `CALL`); an unparsed statement keeps `SQL_DIAG_UNKNOWN_STATEMENT`, which is what that code is *for*. Only the searched forms are reported — the mock has no positioned-update path, so `SQL_DIAG_DYNAMIC_UPDATE` would be a lie. **`SQL_DIAG_CURSOR_ROW_COUNT`** is set from the result-set size for a cursor-opening statement and left at 0 otherwise. **`SQL_DIAG_ROW_COUNT`** was hard-zero for the separate reason **D14** names, fixed here because the two are indistinguishable from outside: `StatementHandle` declared its own `SQLLEN row_count_` that shadowed `OdbcHandle::row_count_`, so `SQLRowCount` wrote the derived field and `SQLGetDiagField` read the base field. The shadow is deleted and the base field widened to `SQLLEN`, which is what both APIs deal in; `cursor_row_count_` was widened for the same reason (it was declared `SQLINTEGER` and read through an `SQLLEN*`). **Locking:** `SQLGetDiagRec` and `SQLGetDiagField` now take a `HandleLock`. The README exempts them from *clearing* the queue, not from locking it — reading `diagnostics_` while another thread posts to the same handle is a data race on a `std::vector`. **Tests:** new `mock-driver/tests/test_diag_header.cpp`, 9 cases covering all four fields plus the `SQL_DIAG_ROW_COUNT` == `SQLRowCount` identity. **Proved:** reverting `mock-driver/src` fails 7 of the 9. **The tool-side probe was also strengthened**, because `test_diagfield_row_count` only checked that the read *succeeded* — a driver answering every read with 0 passed it, which is precisely what the mock did. It now cross-checks `SQL_DIAG_ROW_COUNT` against `SQLRowCount`, which must agree for the same statement whatever the driver's data looks like. **Proved:** against the pre-fix mock the probe FAILs with `SQL_DIAG_ROW_COUNT = 0; SQLRowCount = 10`. **Not covered by any probe:** the tool never reads `SQL_DIAG_RETURNCODE`, `SQL_DIAG_DYNAMIC_FUNCTION` or `SQL_DIAG_CURSOR_ROW_COUNT` at all — a real coverage gap, filed as **B11** rather than folded in here, since adding probes moves the advertised total and belongs with the rest of the §8.1 work. | b7c2d34 |
| D23 | ✅ DONE | HIGH | Memory safety | `SQLGetInfoW` copies the numeric result into the caller's buffer with no bounds check | One-line fix as the row said, plus the SQLSTATE it needed. `unicode_wrappers.cpp`'s numeric path was a bare `std::memcpy(rgbInfoValue, ansi_buf, ansi_len)` that never consulted `cbInfoValueMax`, so `SQLGetInfoW(dbc, SQL_GETDATA_EXTENSIONS, &two_byte_var, 2, &len)` wrote four bytes into a two-byte variable — and since the `.def` exports only `SQLGetInfoW`, that is the path a Unicode driver manager takes on Windows, so the overrun was on the reachable path rather than the dead one. **The fix is not a truncating copy.** Half of a `SQLUINTEGER` is not a smaller number, it is a different one, so a caller that supplies too small a buffer for a fixed-size attribute gets `SQL_ERROR` with **HY090** and an untouched buffer rather than a partial write. **Test**: `GetInfoWRefusesAnUndersizedNumericBuffer` passes a two-byte variable followed by a guard word, and asserts all three things that matter — the return code, the SQLSTATE read back through `SQLGetDiagRec`, and that the guard is intact. The guard is the assertion that would have caught the original bug; the other two describe the replacement. | ca5dc55 |
| D24 | ✅ DONE | HIGH | Spec conformance | Truncation is silent across the whole non-statement API — `01004` is never posted | All six callers now post `01004`, and the copy itself is one function. **The shared helper first** — `utils/buffer_copy.{hpp,cpp}`, part of **D18**: `copy_chars` and `copy_wchars` take an `offset`, always terminate, never write past the buffer, tolerate a null target or zero length (both legal ways to ask only for the length), and report *bytes still available* rather than bytes written, which is what belongs in `StrLen_or_IndPtr`. Six of the eight hand-rolled copies are gone. **The six callers**: `SQLDescribeCol` and `SQLDriverConnect`'s `OutConnectionString` and `SQL_ATTR_CURRENT_CATALOG` discarded `copy_string_to_buffer`'s return code entirely; `SQLColAttribute` and `SQLGetCursorName` hand-rolled the copy and signalled nothing at all, not even a return code a caller could have inspected; `SQLNativeSql` returned `SQL_SUCCESS_WITH_INFO` with an empty diagnostic stack. **A second bug the test found, which the row does not name**: `SQLGetCursorName` returned plain `SQL_SUCCESS` on truncation. Posting `01004` beside a `SQL_SUCCESS` changes nothing an application can see — a driver manager only surfaces the diagnostic stack when the call says there is something in it, which the instrumented probe showed directly (`rc=0 len=20 name='SQ' state=''`). It returns `SQL_SUCCESS_WITH_INFO` now, and the same probe reads `rc=1 … state='01004'`. That is the difference between a fix and a fix that works. **Also fixed here, not named in any row**: `copy_string_to_buffer` computed `SQLSMALLINT src_len = src.length()`. At 32768 bytes that wraps negative, `*string_length` reports a negative length, and the truncation test `src_len >= buffer_length` then answers **false** — so the one case the check exists for was the case it got wrong. Proved by restoring the old arithmetic: the reported length came back as `-25536`, the return code as `SQL_SUCCESS`, and nothing was written. The arithmetic is `SQLLEN` inside `copy_chars` now; only the reported length is narrowed, and it saturates rather than wrapping. **Test**: `mock-driver/tests/test_buffer_copy.cpp`, 14 cases. Ten pin the helper's decisions — the reserved terminator byte, `remaining` being what is available rather than what fit, a null target reporting the length without writing, a zero-length ask for an *empty* value not being a warning, the offset walking a value in chunks with `remaining` counting down, and a surrogate pair never split across two chunks. Four drive real entry points through the driver manager and read the SQLSTATE back with `SQLGetDiagRec`, which is the only way to show that what was posted is what an application can see. | ca5dc55 |
| D25 | ✅ DONE | HIGH | Spec conformance | `SQLProcedureColumns` returns 8 of the 19 required columns | **Done — the named omission and all three related gaps.** **(1) The 19 columns.** `SQLProcedureColumns` emitted the first 8 and stopped, so a client reading by ordinal — which is what the spec's column numbers are for — read the wrong column or ran off the end. The other eleven are filled with values derived the same way the rest of the catalog derives them: `BUFFER_LENGTH` is the transfer size in bytes (doubled for the wide types), `NUM_PREC_RADIX` is 10 for exact numerics and NULL otherwise, `SQL_DATA_TYPE` equals `DATA_TYPE` for every type the mock exposes (none is a datetime interval, the only case where they differ), and `ORDINAL_POSITION` counts the parameter's position in the *procedure* rather than in the result set — so a `COLUMN_NAME` filter does not renumber it, and a function's return value is position 0 by definition. **(2) The search-pattern escape.** `matches_pattern` ignored the `\` the driver advertises through `SQLGetInfo(SQL_SEARCH_PATTERN_ESCAPE)`, so a caller asking for the table literally named `P_B` was handed every three-letter name beginning `P` and ending `B`. An escaped character now matches itself and nothing else. **(3) `SQL_ATTR_METADATA_ID`.** Zero hits repo-wide: a client that set it still got pattern matching, which is the one thing the attribute exists to turn off. Stored on the statement, wired to both accessors, and honoured by the catalog functions through a new `catalog_name_matches()` that switches between pattern matching and case-insensitive identifier equality. **(4) NULL versus the empty string.** The W wrappers collapsed the two with `X.empty() ? nullptr : …` at **37 argument positions** across the ten catalog wrappers — more than the row's estimate — and the ANSI side could not have told them apart anyway, since `sql_to_string` maps both to `""`. Added `sql_to_optional`, and `SQLTables` now reads all four arguments as optionals and implements the three enumeration modes that distinction exists for: `SQL_ALL_CATALOGS`, `SQL_ALL_SCHEMAS` and `SQL_ALL_TABLE_TYPES`, each signalled by `%` in one argument and an *empty string* in the others. A null pointer still means "no filter", which is what every ordinary caller passes and what a test pins down explicitly. **Tests:** new `mock-driver/tests/test_catalog_semantics.cpp`, 13 cases, including two guards against over-correcting — an unescaped `_` must still be a wildcard, and null arguments must still mean no filter. **Proved:** reverting `mock-driver/src` fails 10 of the 13. Mock suite 262 → 275; reference run unmoved at 188 PASS + 18 INFORMATIONAL in Debug and Release. | e35b8c1 |
| D26 | ✅ DONE | MED | Config | `FailOn` is inert unless `Mode=Partial`, and the mock's README does not say so | **Done — honoured, not documented away.** The row offered a choice; `FailOn` names *functions*, so it is a per-function override rather than a property of a mode, and `should_fail` now checks it first whatever the mode is. That also makes a named list *narrow* `Failure` and `Random`: naming a function means "this one", so the others stop failing, which is what a caller writing `Mode=Failure;FailOn=SQLTables` is asking for. Both READMEs were wrong in different ways and both are fixed: the top-level one implied `FailOn` needs `Partial`, and `mock-driver/README.md` omitted `Partial` from the mode list entirely. **Tests:** four cases in `test_config.cpp` — `FailOnIsHonouredInSuccessMode`, `FailOnIsCaseInsensitive`, `ModeFailureStillFailsEverythingWithoutFailOn` (the guard against over-correcting) and `FailOnNarrowsFailureMode`. **Proved:** reverting `mock-driver/src` fails the first two. | f27c9ea |
| D27 | ✅ DONE | MED | Config | Connection-string parser edge cases the 11 adversarial tests miss | **Done — all four.** **(1) `in_braces` was a bool set by a `{` anywhere**, not only at value start, so `Catalog=a{b;Mode=Failure` swallowed the separator and `Mode` was never parsed. Braces are only quoting when the `{` is the first character of a value, which is what ODBC says and what the parser now checks. **(2) An unterminated `{` absorbed the whole tail**, losing every pair after it. The parser looks ahead for a closing `}` before treating one as quoting; without a closer the `{` is an ordinary character, which loses one malformed value instead of all of them. **(3) `}}` is the ODBC escape for a literal `}`** and was unhandled, so `PWD={a}}b}` closed at the first `}` and ate the next pair. Handled in both the scan and the new `unbrace_value()`, which un-doubles it. **(4) `Latency` read any non-`ms` suffix as milliseconds** — `Latency=10s` meant 10 ms, three orders of magnitude out, and `Latency=500us` truncated to 0 because the field was `std::chrono::milliseconds`. The field is microseconds now and the suffix is matched at the end of the string; a bare number is still milliseconds, as the READMEs document. Both READMEs updated with the units. **Tests:** `ConfigTest.ParseLatencyUnits` (four units) plus four parser cases, including `AQuotedValueStillLosesItsBraces` as the guard against over-correcting — that is how every connection string in the suite names the driver. `ConfigTest.ParseLatency` was rewritten to compare durations rather than a raw count, since the field's unit changed deliberately. **Proved:** reverting `mock-driver/src` fails 6 of the 8 new cases. | f27c9ea |
| D28 | ✅ DONE | MED | Transactions | No transaction state exists, and `SQL_ROLLBACK` discards committed rows | **Done — all five.** `ConnectionHandle` gains `in_transaction_`, set when a statement executes with autocommit OFF (ODBC's model is implicit — there is no `BEGIN`) and cleared by `SQLEndTran` or by restoring autocommit. **(1) The rollback that ate committed rows.** `SQLEndTran(SQL_ROLLBACK)` called `clear_inserted_data()` unconditionally, autocommit-ON included — where a rollback should do nothing at all. A probe that inserted rows in autocommit mode and then rolled back lost them, so the fixture whose entire purpose is validating "the rows persisted" assertions was itself deleting the rows. Rollback now discards only what an open transaction accumulated. **(2) `SQLDisconnect` never returned `25000`**, because there was no state to consult; an application that forgot to commit was told everything was fine. **(3) `SQLSetConnectAttr(SQL_ATTR_AUTOCOMMIT)` merely stored the value.** The spec is explicit that OFF → ON commits the open transaction; without it, an application that finished its work and turned autocommit back on left the transaction dangling, and a later rollback discarded rows it had every reason to think were committed. **(4) Any `fType` that was not `SQL_ROLLBACK` was treated as a commit** — a typo in the caller's argument silently committed. `HY012` now, via a new `sqlstate::INVALID_TRANSACTION_OPERATION_CODE`. **(5) The `should_fail` check ran before handle validation**, so with fault injection armed a bad handle came back `SQL_ERROR` — the one return code that says "the handle was fine, the call was not". Validation comes first; `SQLEndTran` was restructured so both handle kinds are resolved once at the top. **Tests:** new `mock-driver/tests/test_transaction_state.cpp`, 10 cases. The last one is a separate fixture-free test because `BehaviorController` is process-global: it connects with `Mode=Partial;FailOn=SQLEndTran`, checks that a statement handle still gets `SQL_INVALID_HANDLE` while a good handle gets the injected `SQL_ERROR`, and restores `Mode=Success` before returning. **Proved:** reverting `mock-driver/src` fails 5 of the 10 — one per defect, including the ordering one, which is invisible unless fault injection is armed. Mock suite 275 → 285; reference run unmoved at 188 PASS + 18 INFORMATIONAL in Debug and Release. | 348f831 |
| D29 | ✅ DONE | MED | Spec conformance | Descriptor and attribute entry points return success for things they do not implement | **Done — every item.** **(1) `SQLColAttribute`.** The ten named fields are implemented (`SQL_DESC_COUNT`, `OCTET_LENGTH`, `TYPE_NAME`, `LABEL`, `TABLE_NAME`/`BASE_TABLE_NAME`, `BASE_COLUMN_NAME`, `UNNAMED`, `CASE_SENSITIVE`, `SEARCHABLE`), and anything still unhandled is `HY091` instead of `SQL_SUCCESS` with a zeroed output — an application cannot tell "the answer is 0" from "I do not know that field". One correction to the row: `SQL_DESC_CONCISE_TYPE` was **not** missing. Its value is 2, the same as `SQL_COLUMN_TYPE`, so the existing case answered it all along; the compiler found the duplicate. **(2) `SQLGetDescField` / `SQLSetDescField`** report `HY091` for unhandled fields — and **making that honest immediately turned two `SQL_C_NUMERIC` probes red**, which is the finding the change was for. The per-record fields were never implemented at all: `SQL_DESC_TYPE`, `CONCISE_TYPE`, `PRECISION`, `SCALE`, `LENGTH`, `OCTET_LENGTH`, `DATA_PTR`, `INDICATOR_PTR`, `OCTET_LENGTH_PTR` were accepted and discarded, so the `test_numeric_struct_roundtrip_byte_equality` and `test_decimal_sum_loop_precision` probes had been passing against a driver that ignored every field they set — configuring an ARD is the only way to bind `SQL_C_NUMERIC` with a chosen precision and scale. All nine now store, and `SQLGetDescField` reads them back; a field an application can set and not read is only half implemented. **(3) `SQLGetDescRec`** returns the record's name instead of discarding it. **(4) The cursor name.** `SQLSetCursorName` stores what it is given and `SQLGetCursorName` returns it, so set-then-get round-trips; the fallback is a monotonic per-statement ordinal rather than the handle address, which used to leak a heap pointer into text applications put into SQL. **(5) `MaxConnections` off by one.** The check was `env->connections_.size() >= max`, but the `ConnectionHandle` constructor has already pushed `this` — so `MaxConnections=1` rejected the very first connect. It counts connections that are *established* now, never the one being established. **(6) `SQLGetFunctions`** no longer claims `SQL_API_SQLSETPOS` or `SQL_API_SQLBULKOPERATIONS`, both of which always answer `HYC00`; a client that trusts `SQLGetFunctions` — which is what it is for — was being sent to call a function the driver had already decided to refuse. **(7) `SQLGetInfo`** answers `SQL_QUOTED_IDENTIFIER_CASE`, `SQL_CORRELATION_NAME`, `SQL_KEYWORDS`, `SQL_MAX_STATEMENT_LEN` and the four `*_CURSOR_ATTRIBUTES2` types instead of `HY096`, which is what a driver says about a type it does not *recognise*. `SQL_MAX_IDENTIFIER_LEN` was already answered — a second correction to the row, again caught by the compiler. **Tests:** new `mock-driver/tests/test_descriptor_contract.cpp`, 15 cases; the `MaxConnections` one is fixture-free because `BehaviorController` is process-global, and it restores `Mode=Success` before returning. **Proved:** reverting `mock-driver/src` fails 13 of the 15. Mock suite 285 → 300; reference run unmoved at 188 PASS + 18 INFORMATIONAL in Debug and Release. | 1eefd16 |
| D32 | ✅ DONE | MED | Fault injection | `SQLConnect` is a fault-injection blind spot and resets the global config | Both halves. **The clobbering**: `SQLConnect` default-constructed a `DriverConfig` and pushed it into the controller, so a DSN-style connect on *any* handle silently discarded a configuration a previous `SQLDriverConnect` had installed. There is no DSN registry here to read a configuration from, so it now reads the existing one and leaves it alone — a caller that has said nothing has not asked for defaults. **The blind spot**: it consulted neither `should_fail`, `apply_latency` nor `max_connections`, all three of which its sibling does, so `FailOn=SQLConnect` and `Latency=` were inert and the DSN path could not be fault-injected at all. All three are there now, in the same order `SQLDriverConnect` uses. **Test**: the two `DISABLED_` tests in `test_connection.cpp` run, **with their assertions unchanged** — which is what makes dropping the prefix the regression test this row asked for rather than a new test written to match the fix. They were disabled for exactly as long as the defect existed. The in-file comments that explained the disabling now explain what changed, because a comment describing a bug the code no longer has is worse than none. | 8964b3d |
| D33 | ✅ DONE | MED | Config | `BufferValidation` is parsed and then never read | `BufferValidation=Lenient` now makes `SQLGetInfo` return string values **without** their NUL terminator, on both the ANSI path (a new `info_return_string` helper behind the `RETURN_STRING` macro in `info_api.cpp`) and the `SQLGetInfoW` path (which is the only one Windows exposes, since the `.def` exports only the W variant). `Strict` remains the default and is byte-for-byte unchanged, so no existing caller is affected. **Brought forward from Phase 5 into Phase 2**, because Phase 2's exit criterion is "each fixed probe has a mock configuration that proves it can now fail" and **A5** had none. **Tests**: `mock-driver/tests/test_fault_injection.cpp` asserts Strict terminates and reports a matching length, and that Lenient leaves no NUL anywhere in a 256-byte buffer while still delivering the value; plus an e2e scenario (`NullTerminationProbeCatchesAnUnterminatedString`) showing the probe PASSes at `Mode=Success` and FAILs under `BufferValidation=Lenient`. Both READMEs now describe what the knob does rather than the "Buffer-size enforcement on input parameters" it never implemented. Note the knob is deliberately scoped to `SQLGetInfo` rather than every string copy in the driver: broadening it would break unrelated paths, and `SQLGetInfo` is where the probe that needs it looks. | 6ccab56 |
| D34 | ✅ DONE | MED | Fault injection | The mock cannot make a numeric value come back wrong | Added two `SilentCorruption` modes and a shared `apply_numeric_skew(value, FetchPath)` helper in `mock/behaviors.{hpp,cpp}`, wired into all **six** places a numeric cell is delivered: the `long long` and `double` arms of the bound-column loop in `SQLFetch` (`statement_api.cpp`), the same two arms in `SQLGetData`, and the two in `SQLFetchScroll`'s verbatim copy of that loop (`driver_main.cpp` — a duplication **D18** will remove, at which point this collapses to two call sites). `SkewNumeric` returns every numeric cell +1 on both paths; `SkewNumericBound` skews only the bound path, so an application reading one column both ways sees the two disagree. Everything else, including the default, is untouched. **Brought forward from Phase 5 into Phase 2** for the same reason as **D33**. **Tests**: 5 cases in `mock-driver/tests/test_fault_injection.cpp` — no skew by default on either path, `SkewNumeric` skews both identically, `SkewNumericBound` skews only the bound path, and an unrelated mode (`MangleVarchar`) leaves numerics alone so the new modes cannot leak into existing scenarios. Plus two e2e scenarios (**A6**, **A7**). **Measured reach**: `SkewNumeric` alone takes the suite from 195/0 to 158 pass / 33 fail against the mock, which is a useful independent signal — 33 probes assert a specific numeric value and were previously untestable, and the lever is now available to Phases 3 and 4. | 6ccab56 |
| D35 | ✅ DONE | MED | Fault injection | The mock could not warn mid-result-set, so no fetch loop was testable | Added while doing **A11**. `SQLFetch` only ever returned `SQL_SUCCESS` or `SQL_NO_DATA`, so nothing could distinguish a loop written `SQLFetch(h) == SQL_SUCCESS` from one written `SQL_SUCCEEDED(SQLFetch(h))` — which is exactly why **14** of them across five probe files had the former. New knob `FetchReturnsWarning=true`: every `SQLFetch` that returns a row also posts `01004` and returns `SQL_SUCCESS_WITH_INFO`. Real drivers do warn per row (truncation, `01S07` fractional truncation, or a driver-specific `01000`) and an application must keep fetching until `SQL_NO_DATA`. **Measured**: with the old loops the suite drops from 195/195 to **188 pass / 1 fail / 6 skip** under this knob, including the exact false FAIL the plan predicted (`test_forward_only_past_end`: "Fetched 0 rows, then SQLFetch returned 1"); with the fixed loops it stays 195/195. **Tests**: two mock unit cases (every row carries `01004` and the cursor still ends at `SQL_NO_DATA` with all rows delivered; quiet by default) plus the e2e scenario for **A11**. | 8bf27eb |
| D36 | ✅ DONE | MED | Fault injection | **Fault injection reaches 18 of the mock's 65 entry points** — the fixture cannot make most of the ODBC surface fail, so most probes have no negative test | **Done — 18 of 65 entry points becomes 46.** Not a blanket "add it everywhere", as the row asks: handle allocation and freeing are excluded by name, because a `FailOn` on `SQLAllocHandle` or `SQLFreeHandle` breaks the harness before a probe runs, and the ODBC 2 aliases are excluded because they delegate. What was added is the surface probes assert against — the whole descriptor API (`SQLGetDescField`, `SQLSetDescField`, `SQLGetDescRec`, `SQLSetDescRec`, `SQLColAttribute`), which had **no** lever at all; the diagnostic readers `SQLGetDiagRec` and `SQLGetDiagField`, which **A23** and the error-queue probes had no way to be proven wrong without; the statement metadata and cursor calls (`SQLNumResultCols`, `SQLDescribeCol`, `SQLBindCol`, `SQLBindParameter`, `SQLRowCount`, `SQLCloseCursor`, `SQLMoreResults`, `SQLNumParams`, `SQLDescribeParam`, `SQLGetStmtAttr`, `SQLSetStmtAttr`, `SQLFetchScroll`); `SQLGetInfo`, `SQLGetFunctions`, `SQLNativeSql`; `SQLDisconnect`, `SQLSetConnectAttr`; and the cursor-name pair. **The diagnostic readers post nothing when they fail** — a reader that fails must not add to the queue it was asked to read. **One measured limit, and one correction to what I first recorded.** `SQLSetCursorName` cannot be made to fail *through the Windows driver manager*: instrumented and confirmed — the driver is reached and returns `SQL_ERROR`, and the application still sees `SQL_SUCCESS`, because the DM maintains cursor names itself. That is the **A23** class, a fact about the stack rather than the driver, so the guard stays for unixODBC and the assertion does not; the comment sits at the guard as well as here. **I first wrote that the same was true of `SQLGetCursorName`, and it is not.** That reading came from a bug in this row's own change: the script that inserted the guards anchored on each function's `clear_diagnostics()` call, `SQLGetCursorName` has none, and the fallback anchor put its guard inside `SQLFetchScroll` instead — so `FailOn=SQLGetCursorName` silently failed `SQLFetchScroll` and left `SQLGetCursorName` unguarded. Found by reading `SQLFetchScroll` for **D18** and seeing a guard that did not belong to it. Guard moved, and `FaultReachTest.GetCursorNameCanBeMadeToFail` now asserts what actually happens: `SQLGetCursorName` *does* surface its error. **A defect found while doing this**: `SQLNativeSql` cast its `SQLINTEGER` length to `SQLSMALLINT`, the same truncation **D14** fixed at two sites in `statement_api.cpp` and did not name here — a 32 KB statement came back as an empty string. Fixed. **Tests:** new `mock-driver/tests/test_fault_injection_reach.cpp`, 5 cases, including `NamingOneFunctionDoesNotFailTheRest` as the guard against over-correcting. **Proved:** reverting `mock-driver/src` fails all 5. | 48b20b9 |
| D37 | ✅ DONE | HIGH | Conformance | **`SQLGetData` never continued a character column** — it restarted the value from byte 0 on every call, so a caller doing what the ODBC spec prescribes looped forever | Found by **A19**, which needed a driver that could return a value longer than one buffer. The character branch of `statement_api.cpp`'s `SQLGetData` copied from `value.c_str()` on every call and reported the *whole* column length as bytes-still-available, so a caller looping while the driver says 01004 — the prescribed way to retrieve a long value — got the same first bytes forever. That made a Core-level conformance behaviour untestable against the reference fixture, and it is the reason `verify_rows_persisted` could ship with a 256-byte read for years. **Fix**: `StatementHandle` carries `getdata_col_` / `getdata_row_` / `getdata_offset_`; `SQLGetData` serves from the offset, reports bytes remaining *as of that call*, and returns `SQL_NO_DATA` once the value is exhausted. The offset is keyed on (column, row) and checked inside `SQLGetData` itself, so every path that moves the cursor — `SQLFetch`, `SQLFetchScroll`, re-execute, `SQLFreeStmt(SQL_CLOSE)`, the catalog functions — restarts the value without any of them having to remember to reset anything. **Test**: the five mock-backed cases in `tests/unit/test_row_verification.cpp`; the 700-character round trip is impossible without this. Proven by reverting the offset advance and watching them fail. **Not done**: a `FailOn`-style knob that makes the mock deliberately *not* advance, so the caller-side guard has a permanent regression test rather than the one-off manual proof recorded in A19. That belongs with **D36**, Phase 5 order 40. | 1b40f69 |
| D38 | ✅ DONE | LOW | Conformance | The mock advertises `SQL_IDENTIFIER_QUOTE_CHAR` as `\"` but its SQL parser cannot read a quoted identifier | **Done.** The parser took the identifier as the raw token, quotes included, so `SELECT COUNT(*) FROM "ODBC_TEST_PARAM"` found no table while the driver advertised `"` as `SQL_IDENTIFIER_QUOTE_CHAR`. Added `unquote_identifier()`, which strips a matching pair and un-doubles an escaped `""` inside, and routed all six `result.table_name` assignments through it — before `to_upper`, not after, or the fold would have run on a name that still had its quotes. **A correction to D29, made here:** D29 answered `SQL_QUOTED_IDENTIFIER_CASE` with `SQL_IC_SENSITIVE` on the assumption that quoted names keep their case. They do not — the mock folds every identifier to upper case, quoted or not, which is exactly `SQL_IC_UPPER`. Leaving `SENSITIVE` would have replaced one unhonoured capability claim with another, which is the shape this row is about. **Tests:** three new cases in `test_catalog_semantics.cpp`, covering a quoted SELECT, a quoted INSERT and DELETE, and the claim-versus-behaviour agreement (`SQL_IC_UPPER` reported *and* a lower-case quoted name resolving). **Proved:** reverting `mock-driver/src` fails all three. | cc3dc0c |
| D39 | ✅ DONE | MED | Install scripts | `register.ps1` wrote an explicitly supplied `-DriverPath` to the registry verbatim, so a relative path registered a driver that only loaded from one directory | The auto-detect branches resolved with `Resolve-Path`; the explicit `-DriverPath` branch did not. This repo's own Appendix passes a relative path (`-DriverPath mock-driver/build/Debug/mockodbc.dll`), so the registry held `mock-driver/build/Debug/mockodbc.dll` and the Driver Manager resolved it against each process's working directory. `odbc-crusher.exe` run from the repo root loaded the driver and reported 195/195; the CTest suites, which run from `build/tests`, could not, and **every mock-backed unit test skipped itself with “Mock ODBC Driver not registered on this host”** — a silent skip that looked exactly like a clean run. Found while adding **A19**'s tests, which passed when run by hand and skipped under `ctest`. **Fix**: one line — `Resolve-Path` the explicit path too, so the branch agrees with the other two. **Test**: the unit suite went from 66 tests to 73 with no test added beyond A19's, because seven that had been skipping now run; `ctest -L unit` reporting 73 is the regression signal. Distinct from **D30**, which is about `register.sh` idempotency on Linux and stays open. | 1b40f69 |
| D40 | ✅ DONE | MED | Fault injection | A `SQLEndTran(SQL_COMMIT)` that returns `SQL_ERROR` left its rows visible, so no probe could distinguish a failed commit from a successful one | Found while giving **A22** a test. `transaction_api.cpp` returned `SQL_ERROR` from the `should_fail("SQLEndTran")` branch and left `inserted_data_` untouched, so a probe that INSERTs, commits and then verifies persistence still found every row — the fixture could not produce the one shape A22 exists to report. A failed COMMIT now clears the inserted data, which in the mock's model is what "the transaction did not commit" means. A failed ROLLBACK is deliberately left alone: there the rows may legitimately still be present. **Tested — and this row first said it could not be.** Measured with a trace through the mock, only 6 of a full run's `SQLEndTran` calls reach the driver on **Windows**: the DM answers the rest itself, because the probes that verify persistence run in autocommit, where there is no commit to fail. The conclusion drawn from that — "no current probe observes this change" — was wrong, because it generalised one driver manager to all of them. **unixODBC forwards `SQLEndTran` in autocommit**, so on ubuntu-22.04 and macOS this change is what makes `test_bindparam_int_to_varchar_roundtrip`, `test_param_rebind_per_row_row_count` and `test_param_batch_then_single_row_tail` find `COUNT(*) = 0` and report the commit as the cause. CI is what caught the error. The e2e scenario `FailedCommitIsBlamedOnTheCommitNotTheBindPath` asserts that wherever the commit does reach the driver, the report names it and does not blame the bind path. | 3ece494 |
| D41 | ✅ DONE | HIGH | Conformance | **`SELECT 1 FROM T` was a `42S22`** — the mock could not select a constant from a table, so the table-existence probe and every one of A2's dialect variants were unreachable against it | Found while giving **A15** a test, which failed to fail: the reuse path A15 fixes was never taken against the mock, so removing the fix changed nothing. The cause is that every select-list item was looked up as a column name, and anything else came back `42S22 "Column not found: 1"`. Two consequences, both of them the same "the fixture cannot exercise the code" shape as **D33**/**D34**/**D35**. **(1)** `SELECT 1 FROM <table> WHERE 1=0` is the existence probe both `create_test_table()` helpers run before reusing a table, so that whole branch was dead here and A15 could not be regression-tested. **(2)** Every dialect variant **A2** builds — `SELECT <expr> FROM RDB$DATABASE` for Firebird, `SELECT <expr> FROM DUAL` for Oracle — is a constant selected from a table, so A2's own Details note that "the Firebird branch is never taken here" was understating it: those queries could not have run against this driver at all. **Fix**, in `mock_data.cpp`: a select-list item that parses as an integer, a decimal or a single-quoted string becomes a constant column of the matching SQL type, named after the expression; anything else still gets its 42S22. The projection loop was rebuilt around a per-item plan, because the old one collected column *indices* and silently dropped what it could not resolve — which would have shifted every later column one place left in `SELECT ID, 7, V`. A constant WHERE predicate is evaluated too: `WHERE 1=0` had no column on its left, so the filter was skipped entirely and the statement returned every row, the exact opposite of what it asks for. **Test**: `mock-driver/tests/test_literal_select.cpp`, 8 cases — the existence probe succeeds and returns nothing, still fails for a table that does not exist, integer and string constants repeat per row, a constant keeps its position among real columns, an unknown column is still 42S22, `WHERE 1=1` keeps every row, and a column predicate still filters. The last three exist because each is a way the fix could be wrong while the first ones passed. | 7c0971a |

ASan installs its handlers when the process starts; ours are installed later, per category, and therefore **win**. A genuine memory error inside a probe is caught by our `siglongjmp`, the category is replaced by one `(DRIVER CRASH)` entry (**D63**), and ASan is never given the chance to print. The sanitizer build has been blind to any fault inside a test category for as long as the guard has existed. **`ODBC_CRUSHER_NO_CRASH_GUARD=1`** runs the body without installing handlers, so whatever is underneath - ASan, or the platform's own fault reporting - gets to report. Off by default, on **both** platforms rather than only the one that needs it today, because a switch that exists on one OS is a switch that gets forgotten on the other. The guard stays the default: it is what keeps one driver crash from taking a whole run down, which is the tool's stated "never crash" contract. **The `[D57]` step now produces evidence rather than a name.** It prints the crash entry's `actual` - which carries the signal, and `SIGABRT` (a fortified libc catching an overrun) and `SIGSEGV` (a bad pointer) are different faults with different fixes - and then re-runs the crashing category alone with the guard off so ASan reports it properly. **The re-run had to be widened.** The first version re-ran the crashing category alone with `--category`, and it finished cleanly - which is its own finding: the fault needs state earlier categories build up.
